The meshes were authored in world space and then ALSO given the pedestal's yaw, so they were rotated a second time. S2 (yaw 0) was unaffected, which is why it had never been noticed.
- Why it matters: three 47cm dark stone plates floating at z≈0.95 in the middle of the entrance floor, in shot for any wide frame of the hall.
- Fix applied: zeroed `rotation_euler` on the three affected objects — the mesh data already carries the correct world position, so removing the duplicate rotation puts each back on its pedestal.
- Verified: re-measured world bounds for all four; each inlay now centres on its own pedestal.
- Flagged for review: no.

## [2026-08-02 ~17:30 IST] Baked GI + web export: four silent-failure traps in the Blender API
- Context: building `tools/blender/bake_lightmap.py` (decimate → lightmap UV atlas → Cycles GI bake) and `export_web_interior.py`. Recording the traps because each FAILED SILENTLY or reported success, and all four will bite anyone touching these scripts.
- **1. `nodes.new()` returns a pointer that goes stale.** Arming the bake target with `for nd in nt.nodes: nd.select = (nd is n)` matched nothing, because touching the node tree invalidates the reference `nodes.new()` handed back. The bake target was therefore never selected, Cycles baked a completely black atlas, and the only clue was an info-level "No active and selected image texture node" line buried in the log AFTER the whole bake had run. Compare node NAMES, not identities. The script now asserts the target is armed on every material and raises before baking.
- **2. The depsgraph does not evaluate modifiers on hidden objects.** Decimating `table_client` returned the original mesh unchanged while printing a plausible ratio (`18112->18112|ratio=0.331`). The object is `hide_viewport`, so the depsgraph never visits it. The decimator now unhides, evaluates, and restores.
- **3. Assigning `colorspace_settings.name` after writing `img.pixels` invalidates the buffer** and saves a black file. The sRGB transfer is now applied explicitly in numpy and the image left Non-Color, so Blender writes the numbers verbatim.
- **4. Linear 8-bit is the wrong container for a lightmap.** Normalised against its 99.5th percentile the room sits near 0.05 — value ≈13 in linear 8-bit, which bands visibly. The atlas is sRGB-encoded for precision and the divisor (4.6597) is published in the manifest as `lightMapIntensity`.
- Also: `smart_project`'s own packer left most of the square empty (147 objects of cornice and moulding produce mostly thin slivers) — a `pack_islands` pass with `shape_method='CONCAVE'` reclaims it.
- Architectural decision worth recording: **only the 147 unique shell objects are lightmapped.** The heavy repeats are already instanced — 153 anthemions off one 2.5k mesh, 54 balusters off one, 11 capitals off one — and a lightmap needs per-placement UVs, so lightmapping them would have destroyed the instancing that keeps the GPU upload at 214k vertices against 1.44M rendered. Ornament and props are lit at runtime instead.
- Correction to an earlier claim made in this session: unique geometry is **519k triangles, not 2.01M**. The 2.01M figure counts instances. Decimation was therefore applied to the shared DATABLOCKS, so one pass propagates through every instance (the capital mesh went 60k → 10k once and paid off 11 times).
- Verified: 4096 atlas, 1024 samples, OptiX. Drawn triangles 2.01M → 480k. Visual check of the atlas shows the coffered ceiling, wall panels with sconce falloff and the rosette all legible.
- Flagged for review: no.

## [2026-08-02 ~18:20 IST] Three scene defects found only because the exporter tripped over them
- **`EXT_HAZE` shipped in the GLB.** A 480m volumetric box that lives in the Scene Collection, NOT in `COL_Exterior` — so excluding the exterior never dropped it. It blew the exported scene bounds out to ±240m. The export now selects an explicit interior set rather than "everything visible".
- **The `KIT_` instancing masters exported as real geometry.** They are parked at z=−50 as sources and are not hidden, so they shipped as meshes sitting 50m under the floor. Same fix.
- **Both rug materials had the wrong normal map.** They drive a Bump node from `Carpet013_2K-PNG_Displacement` — a 16-bit greyscale HEIGHT map. Cycles is fine with that, but glTF has no bump node, so the exporter wrote the height map into `normalTexture`: wrong for shading, and unreadable to the texture toolchain (libvips rejects `I;16`). Both materials already carry a proper `NormalGL` map, so the export bypasses the bump and wires it through. **Export path only — `mansion_exterior.blend` is untouched.**
- Verified: post-fix scene bounds are 15.6 × 11.6 × 6.5 m (the actual hall); `Carpet013_2K-PNG_NormalGL` now occupies the normalTexture slot.
- Flagged for review: **yes** — the rug fix is applied at export. If the interactive build ever bakes from the .blend directly, fix the material there too.

## [2026-08-02 ~18:50 IST] GLB failed glTF validation; KTX2 toolchain version-mismatched
- Found: **`MAT_Wood_Dark` carried `roughnessFactor` 1.05**, against a spec maximum of 1.0. Clamping the Principled socket in Blender does NOT catch it — the value is not a socket default, it only exists once the exporter flattens the multiply chain that drives roughness. Also one normal-mapped primitive shipped without TANGENT, which the spec warns makes the tangent basis non-portable.
- Why it matters: an invalid asset is a loader-behaviour lottery across engines, and it was about to be committed as the deliverable.
- Fix applied: `tools/gltf/fix_ranges.py` clamps material scalars on the EXPORTED document (roughness, metallic, baseColor/emissive factors, occlusion strength, and the transmission/specular/clearcoat/sheen/iridescence/ior extension factors). `export_tangents=True` added to the exporter.
- Second problem, tooling: **`gltf-transform` 4.4.2 invokes `ktx create --assign-oetf`, which KTX-Software v5 renamed to `--assign-tf`**, so every texture failed to encode. Rather than pin an older KTX, `tools/gltf/encode_ktx2.py` drives `ktx` directly and wires `KHR_texture_basisu` by hand — which also allowed per-slot codec choice the wrapper does not expose usefully: **UASTC for normal maps** (ETC1S quantises a direction field hard enough to facet across large flat walls) and **ETC1S for everything else**, including the 4K atlas, where UASTC cost 7.1MB against 1.14MB for gradients nobody can see.
- Third problem, tooling: `gltf-transform`'s `resize`/`webp` shell out to sharp, and the libvips npx installs on this machine fails on EVERY png with "colourspace: parameter space not set". The texture stage runs in Pillow instead (`tools/gltf/optimize_textures.py`).
- Verified: **Khronos glTF-Validator 2.0.0-dev.3.9 — 0 errors.** All 56 warnings trace to that build predating `KHR_texture_basisu`: it cannot follow references made through the Basis and Draco extensions, so it reports the 28 images and 218 bufferViews they reach as unused and calls `image/ktx2` an invalid mime type. The extension is declared in both `extensionsUsed` and `extensionsRequired`, which is exactly what the spec requires. Recorded in `interior_hall.manifest.json` so it is not re-opened.
- Final: 235MB raw → **14.4MB**. Delivered to `apps/public/public/models/interior_hall.glb` with the Basis and Draco decoders in `public/basis/` and `public/draco/`.
- Flagged for review: **yes** — one primitive still carries a `TEXCOORD_1` its material does not sample. One unused attribute; not worth hand-editing the buffer.

## [2026-08-02 ~19:40 IST] CI had never run on this code; both jobs failed on first execution
- Context: the repo previously contained only a README. Pushing the project's history ran the workflow for the first time. Neither failure was a regression from the 3D work — both were latent, and each was hiding the next.
- **Lint: `next lint` opened its interactive setup prompt on a runner with no TTY.** There was no ESLint config or dependency anywhere in the repo, so `next lint` asked "How would you like to configure ESLint?" and died. Fix: added `eslint` + `eslint-config-next` and a `next/core-web-vitals` config to both apps, then fixed the 11 errors that surfaced — all `react/no-unescaped-entities` in user-facing copy, now proper typographic entities (`&ldquo;`, `&rsquo;`). Remaining findings are warnings (mostly `<img>` vs `next/image`) and do not fail the build.
- **Unit tests would have failed the moment lint was fixed.** Every spec in `packages/db` is an integration test that opens a real Postgres connection and needs both the superuser and crm_app roles — and they THROW AT IMPORT when the URLs are absent. The validate job provisions no database. Fix: `packages/db/vitest.config.ts` collects them only when a database is configured, reading `packages/db/.env` exactly as the specs do so local runs are unchanged.
- Also untracked `*.tsbuildinfo`. TypeScript's incremental build cache is a build artefact; the tracked copies made a rebase abort with "untracked working tree files would be overwritten".
- Verified: ran the full validate job locally with the database env removed, matching the runner — typecheck, lint and test all green. Then green on GitHub Actions.
- Flagged for review: **yes** — `packages/db` now runs ZERO tests in CI. The scaffolding to change that is in place: give the validate job a Postgres service and set `DATABASE_URL_MIGRATIONS` + `DATABASE_URL_CRM` and the specs run unchanged. That is a scope decision, not a CI fix.

## [2026-08-02 ~20:05 IST] The migration set could not build a database from scratch
- Found: provisioning a real Postgres for E2E killed the run on `type "media_kind" already exists`. A static pass over every `CREATE TYPE` / `CREATE TABLE` / `ADD CONSTRAINT` across all 23 migrations found **twelve objects created by more than one migration**, in two real groups plus one false positive.
- Why it matters: nobody could stand up a fresh environment. The schema had only ever existed through incremental application on machines that already had most of it.
- Fix applied: (1) **`0009_media.sql` removed** — it was a duplicate of the core-media half of `0010_media.sql` (same two enums, same table, same five constraints) and, alone among every migration in the repo, carried NO idempotency guards, so it could not even be re-run by itself. `0010` supersedes it and additionally covers the projection side. (2) **`0010_media.sql` guarded** where it re-created three objects `0007_projection.sql` already owns — the `pub_media_kind` enum, the `media_manifests` table and its foreign key — using the same `DO $$ ... EXCEPTION WHEN duplicate_object` pattern the rest of the repo uses, plus `IF NOT EXISTS` on the table and indexes.
- False positive worth recording so it is not "fixed" later: `unit_status_events_legal_transition` appears in both `0003` and `0019`, but `0019` DROPs it before re-adding to widen the check for mortgage states. That one is correct as written.
- Verified: the full migration loop applies cleanly to an empty PostGIS server in CI.
- Flagged for review: **yes** — `apply_migration.ts` keeps no record of what it has applied, so every run re-executes every file. Survivable now the statements are idempotent, but a migrations table would be better. This also supersedes the PROGRESS.md note about "duplicate migration numbers": `0009_media.sql` is gone, so `0009_missing_rls.sql` is now unambiguous; `0010_media.sql` + `0010_office_settings.sql` still share a prefix but create disjoint objects.

## [2026-08-02 ~20:25 IST] E2E: CI was not testing the database shape we deploy, and knocked on the wrong port
- **Migrations then died on `3F000 schema "extensions" does not exist`.** That schema is a Supabase convention — it installs PostGIS there rather than in `public` — and this codebase depends on the layout in two ways: two migrations grant USAGE on it (`0016` to crm_app, `0020` to projection_reader), and the application SQL is schema-qualified in five places (`publish.ts` ST_Extent, `geometry.ts` ST_SetSRID/ST_GeomFromGeoJSON, `projection.ts` ST_AsGeoJSON). A stock `postgis/postgis` image installs into `public` and has no such schema. **Relaxing the grants would only have moved the failure** — every `extensions.ST_*` call the app makes would then fail at runtime, and CI would have gone green against a database shape we never deploy. Fix: `packages/db/ci/bootstrap_extensions.sql` recreates the Supabase layout before the migration loop, so `0000_init`'s `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS postgis` becomes a no-op instead of installing into public first. It lives in `packages/db/ci/`, not `migrations/`, because it describes the managed platform rather than our schema.
- **Then every navigation failed with ERR_CONNECTION_REFUSED.** `apps/public` runs `next dev --port 3001`, and `playwright.config.ts` already health-checks `http://localhost:3001` before starting the suite — so the server was up and healthy while the tests knocked on **3000**. `PLAYWRIGHT_TEST_BASE_URL` was `http://present.localhost:3000`. The mismatch predates all of this work; it was in the workflow from the start. Fix: point it at 3001. The `present.` subdomain is deliberate — the Next middleware keys on it to route into the `(present)` segment, and Chromium resolves `*.localhost` to 127.0.0.1 per RFC 6761, so no hosts entry is needed on the runner.
- Also added `set -e` to the migration loop, which was letting a failed migration continue to the next file.
- Verified: **both CI jobs green on `main`** — `Typecheck, Lint, and Unit Tests` and `Playwright E2E (Merge Blocking)`.
- Flagged for review: no.

## [2026-08-02 ~20:35 IST] Build automation and its inputs were unversioned; repo now has a remote
- Found: the repo had **no git remote at all**, and 20 Blender/glTF pipeline scripts existed only on the local C: drive. The hologram pipeline that produces the S1/S2 displays was entirely unversioned.
- Why it matters: total loss exposure on a single machine, for the automation as well as the output.
- Fix applied: all 20 scripts committed to `tools/blender/` and `tools/gltf/`. Three that had silently drifted ahead of their repo copies were synced (`kit.py` gained `revolve()`, `prep_ornament.py` an explicit ROTX override, `preview_asset.py` a wider framing distance). Pushed to `github.com/Dev-Ritvik/QHR-ecosystem` (private), rebased onto the existing README commit rather than force-pushing over it.
- Also versioned the pipeline's **inputs**, which the scripts alone do not make reproducible: the two layout sheets and the original Kartikeya photograph, by narrowing the `assets/` ignore rule rather than dropping it (`assets/` is 1.9GB and stays out). The issued Lucky Garden PDF is deliberately NOT tracked — 26MB, larger than everything else in the repo combined, and `lucky_garden_raw.png` is the 300dpi render the pipeline actually reads.
- Flagged for review: **yes** — `decimate_chandelier.py` is still the one pipeline script that never received the world-transform bake fix its siblings have (the OBJ/FBX importers express Y-up→Z-up as an OBJECT rotation, so scripts measuring local `me.vertices` compute extents in the wrong frame). Nothing is broken in the scene today — it was corrected downstream by hand — but any asset re-run through it will come out rotated 90 degrees.

## [2026-08-27 22:45 IST] Exterior AO + material export: five defects that were invisible in Blender

- Context: the pre-bake geometry audit closed with `mansion_exterior_GEOMETRY-FINAL_20260827-1618.blend` (sha256 `411e4324…9942008`, re-verified byte-identical to the live file at the start of this session). Everything below is AO, material and export work on top of that lock. No vertex moved: a full re-dump of all 363 masonry objects against the lock file shows **0 bounding-box differences** and an identical loop count (184,632).

- **1. `StoneAO` was dead data, and `EXT_HAZE` is why.** Measured across all 100,679 loops of `mansion_walls`: p10 0.6192, median 0.62, p90 0.62. That flatness is `AO_FLOOR` from `build_stone_material_system.py` — the Cycles AO bake underneath it had returned 0.0 everywhere, so the only surviving signal was the floor plus the analytic ground-dirt ramp (0.434 at z=0 rising to 0.62 by z=1.70). Cause: `EXT_HAZE` is a **480 x 480 x 56 m box centred at z=26 with `hide_render=False`**, enclosing the entire building. Every AO ray hit it. Compounding it, `MAT_Stone_Wall`'s AO multiply sat at Factor **0** while Rustic/Trim/Paving/Steps sat at Factor **1** — so every cornice, portico, fountain and step was being multiplied by a flat ~0.62 carrying no AO information at all, while the wall was multiplied by nothing. The ornament was uniformly 38% muddier than the wall for no reason.

- **2. The Cycles AO bake operator cannot be trusted in this scene — replaced with raycasting.** `cupola_parapet_0` sits at z 7.60-7.96 with open sky above it and nothing within a metre of its outer face; a hemisphere raycast measures it **84% open**. The operator baked it to **0.000** — on GPU and on CPU, selected alone or with the set, with the object's own duplicate hidden, and at AO distances from 0.05 m to 20 m. The same duplicate moved to z 8.60 baked to 0.88 and at z 13.00 to 1.00. A fresh cube in clear air baked correctly (mean 0.861), so the operator is not globally broken; whatever it was integrating at that position was not the geometry that is there. Rather than keep guessing at it, `tools/blender/bake_ao_raycast.py` computes AO by deterministic cosine-weighted hemisphere raycast against an **explicit, auditable occluder set** — the same method every other geometric question in this project was settled with. Spot checks agree with independent measurement: parapet 0.802, west field 0.99, under the portico cornice 0.01, terrace at the wall base 0.552, open terrace 1.00.
  - **The big-face guard matters as much as the bake.** `mansion_walls` carries each facade field as a single n-gon of 57-86 m2; ten polygons are 43.7% of its whole surface. Those n-gons' corners are buried under the terrace and behind the eaves, so a corner-sampled vertex attribute renders the entire visible facade at ~7% brightness — the black-wall failure. Faces over 0.25 m2 are therefore shaded flat from the mean openness of the points on them that are actually **exposed** (a sample whose own outward ray cannot clear 50 mm is buried and gets no vote).
  - Applied masonry is deliberately **not** an occluder. Measured with it included, the four fields come out at 0.406/0.403/0.412/0.406 — all at the floor — but the south field n-gon also spans the smooth entrance bay, which measures 0.479 open inside the portico. One flat value cannot be both a joint bottom and the front door. It is also unnecessary: the runtime key is a shadow-casting directional at 10.6 degrees of elevation whose shadow map is 2048 px over a 52 m box (25 mm/texel), so it was never going to resolve an 18 mm joint whatever AO did.
  - Result, measured ON/OFF in **linear** (EXR, not display-referred): global min 0.404 = exactly the floor, reached by 0.1-0.8% of pixels. Localised where it should be — s6 (portico elevation) 25.1% of pixels below 0.7, hero 6.0%, west6 4.4%.

- **3. The glTF exporter is blind to the legacy Mix node, and it silently cost the tint and the AO.** Blender 5.2 reads base colour by walking back from the Base Color socket for `constant x colour attribute x texture`, and `search_node_tree.get_multiply_factors` matches that **only** through `node.type == 'MIX'` with `data_type == 'RGBA'`. All five stone materials used `ShaderNodeMixRGB`, whose type is `MIX_RGB`, with sockets `Color1`/`Color2` instead of `#A_Color`/`#B_Color`. Every test fails, so the exporter cannot see past the node. Measured on the export:
  - `MAT_Stone_Wall.baseColorFactor = [1, 1, 1, 1]` — the locked #E7E3DA tint (linear 0.799/0.768/0.701) simply gone.
  - `COLOR_0` = 255 on all 85,144 vertices of `mansion_walls`, with the real StoneAO landing in **COLOR_1** (ushort, min 26214/65535 = 0.400, the AO floor exactly). three.js binds COLOR_0 and ignores COLOR_1, so the AO shipped in the file and did nothing. The only warning was one info-level line: "The active Vertex Color will not be exported, as it is not used in the node tree of the material."
  - Fix: `tools/blender/fix_stone_material_export.py` converts them to `ShaderNodeMix` (RGBA/MULTIPLY/factor 1) — bit-for-bit the same arithmetic, so nothing about the Blender render changes. After: `baseColorFactor [0.7991, 0.7682, 0.7011, 1]` and COLOR_0 carrying 223 distinct values from 0.400 to 1.000. It also zeroes `MAT_Gold`'s Anisotropic (0.4 -> 0), which was shipping `KHR_materials_anisotropy` on geometry with no TANGENT — NaN in the anisotropic lobe, smeared by the bloom pass. That was being stripped downstream in `apply_ktx2_safe_textures.py`; it now never leaves Blender, and the extension is absent from the exported asset.

- **4. `export_web.py` would have shipped the mansion with no masonry.** `COL_Ashlar_West/East/North/South` are **siblings** of `COL_Exterior` at scene level — that is how the generator rebuilds them and how `survey_facades.py` hides them — and the exporter gathered `COL_Exterior.objects` non-recursively. A set is now a **list** of collections gathered via `all_objects`, with the parked `*_KIT` masters filtered by name and by z. The shipped `exterior_mansion.glb` shows the same shortfall from the other side: 212 nodes / 114 meshes against the current 479 / 382 — a difference of exactly 267 nodes. **The exterior currently in production predates the entire Phase-2 masonry.**

- **5. The 267 ashlar blocks had local UVs, which is why the wall and the blocks read as different stone.** `build_stone_material_system.py` states the rule the system depends on: "a world-axis box at exactly 1 UV unit per metre, with V on world Z". `mansion_walls` obeys it (u -8.05..8.05). The blocks did not: `ashlar_WEST_028` ran u -0.53..0.53, and across all 267 there were only **30 distinct UV origins**, so at the locked 3.33 m tile every block on the building sampled the same +/-16% window of Marble026, and any two blocks sharing a kit mesh were texturally identical. That is the pale mismatched panel visible around every window at REV_WEST6 and the flat slab at the south-west corner — not a stray object, just two different windows onto the same 2048px marble. `tools/blender/fix_masonry_uvs.py` re-projects the placed blocks in world space. World UVs are a function of world position, so this un-shares the ashlar kit meshes (78 -> 267, +7,560 loop-vertices, no change to draw calls, which were already one per node). The 96 **rustic** blocks were reverted to their 2 shared meshes: un-sharing them costs ~170k loop-vertices for a base course that is legitimately repetitive.
  - Trap worth recording: the two rustic kit meshes are `KIT_quoin_x_M` (long in X) and `KIT_quoin_x_M.001` (long in Y), and every rustic object has rotation (0,0,0) — the 90-degree turn lives in the **mesh**, not the transform. Stripping a `.001` suffix collapsed them into one and silently rotated 42 blocks. Correct mapping, confirmed against the lock and by run direction (f/b run along X at 0.703 m spacing, l/r along Y at 0.695 m): f/b -> `KIT_quoin_x_M`, l/r -> `KIT_quoin_x_M.001`.

- Verified against the exported asset, not the viewport (`tools/gltf/verify_export_qa.py`, new): 479 nodes / 382 meshes / 479 draw calls / 179,664 drawn triangles, 0 orphan nodes, 0 non-uniform or negative scales, 0 non-unit normals sampled, no missing or unreferenced images, `model_min.y = -0.0` (sits on the ground), only `MAT_Glass_Window` non-opaque, and COLOR_0 present on every stone material at 0.400-1.000. Container QA (`verify_final_glb.py`): 9.72 MB, Draco + KTX2 required, 26 images all 1024 square and multiple-of-4, no anisotropy materials, 0 negative texCoords.
- Restore point: `mansion_exterior_AO-MATERIAL_20260827-2243.blend`, sha256 `0e0a2ab1a38df363c9b9a61921231eadad761aa8cc16b611182d454924d840ce`, verified identical to live.
- Flagged for review: **yes** — two things. (a) The new asset is installed as `apps/public/public/models/exterior_mansion_v5.glb`; the production `exterior_mansion.glb` is untouched and `EXTERIOR_MODEL_URL` still points at it, so the swap is a one-line change awaiting sign-off. (b) 9.72 MB against production's 5.62 MB. That is honest cost, not regression: production carried 13 images because it predates the five-material stone system; this carries 26, of which 4.2 MB is UASTC normal maps. If the budget matters more than normal-map fidelity, encoding normals as ETC1S is the lever.

## [2026-08-28 07:55 IST] Exterior v5 shipped: runtime QA found two things Blender and gltf-transform both passed

- Context: `exterior_mansion_v5.glb` had already passed structural export QA. This pass took it through the actual web runtime, which is where the last two defects turned up. `EXTERIOR_MODEL_URL` now points at v5; the previous asset is untouched on disk and still addressable as `?model=prod`, which is the rollback.

- **Triangle-count reconciliation, because a report must not carry two totals.** 179,664 and 179,397 were both in circulation. Cross-tabulating four counting methods against both files (`tools/gltf/reconcile_triangles.py`) shows every method AGREES on each file — the verifiers never disagreed, the two numbers are two different files. The pipeline removes 267 triangles between the raw export and the shipped GLB: 14 cypress meshes at 16 each, `KIT_finial_tip_M` at 8 (shared by 4 nodes), and `mansion_walls` at 11. Measured on the raw export, those meshes contain **exactly 16, 8 and 11 zero-area triangles** respectively — Draco's degenerate-face pass, removing geometry that could never have been seen. `dedup` separately merges `door_handle_1`'s mesh into `door_handle_0` (−44 unique, 0 drawn), which closes the unique-count delta of −287. **Authoritative: 179,397 drawn triangles.**

- **1. `applyGrade` would have painted the rustication with the paving texture.** The paving grade cloned ONE material and assigned it to every mesh matching `/^(terrace_|rustic_|entry_step|entry_cheek|fount_)/` — correct when there was a single `MAT_Stone_Cream` to clone, damage the moment the stone was split. Against v5 it would have taken whichever of MAT_Stone_Paving / MAT_Stone_Rustic / MAT_Stone_Steps / MAT_Stone_Trim it met first and painted all 107 meshes with it, on top of a second darkening over the AO already in COLOR_0. Fixed by keying the clone on the SOURCE material — four clones instead of one, still not one per mesh.
  - Also caught `fount_water`, which is MAT_Water: transmissive, not a horizontal stone plate, and the grade was darkening the fountain. The clone now applies only to `MAT_Stone*`.

- **2. Disabling that grade outright was ALSO wrong, and only a measurement showed it.** The first fix skipped the grade entirely for split-stone assets, on the reasoning that the baked AO now does the job. Sampling the hero frame at fixed world points says otherwise:

    ```
                        production   v5 ungraded   v5 graded
        facade_west          87          94           94
        terrace_left         69         110           83
        terrace_front        56          83           64
    ```

    Ungraded, the terrace climbs ABOVE the west elevation — the same inverted hierarchy the tint was written to correct, with a different asset underneath it. AO answers "how enclosed is this surface"; the question here is "what is a horizontal plate worth against a lit wall at dusk", which occlusion cannot answer. Graded, v5 ranks `facade_south > facade_west > roof > terrace > spire > forecourt`, structurally identical to production and marginally better (the elevation now sits above the roof deck rather than tied with it).

- **UASTC normal maps kept, on measurement, not on principle.** An ETC1S-normals candidate builds to **3.63 MB against 9.72 MB** — the KTX2 payload drops 8.6 MB to 2.4 MB. Across the stone at the 6 m west camera the two are indistinguishable: mean absolute difference **0.425/255**. But the pixels that do differ are not scattered — 12 of the 12 worst 80px cells sit in one horizontal band at the bottom of the frame, which is the terrace paving, the only large flat surface in shot. That is exactly what `encode_ktx2.py` documents when it picks the codec: ETC1S "quantises the endpoints hard enough to produce visible faceting across large flat walls". So the 6 MB is real and so is the reason to leave it. The saving worth having is a MIXED policy — UASTC for `paving_normal` alone, ETC1S for the other seven normals (~6.7 MB of normal maps) — which is a pipeline change rather than a swap, and is left as a measured recommendation. The candidate is kept as `?model=v5etc1s` rather than deleted.

- **Runtime verification, in the browser, through the real page.** The Browser pane could not composite (`document.hidden`, rAF 0 ticks/s, R3F never mounted), so this ran under Playwright at 1440x900 where the canvas is 1424x900 and rAF ticks at 113 Hz. Measured with no query parameter at all, i.e. against the new default:
  - `exterior_mansion_v5.glb`, 9,953 KB, 166 ms; 267 ashlar; 96 rustic; **408 vertex-coloured meshes** against production's 1.
  - `mansion_walls` material live in three: colour **#e7e3da**, roughness 1, metalness 0, **normalScale 1.2**, `vertexColors: true`, COLOR_0 itemSize 3.
  - Glass transparent at opacity 0.55; gold metalness 1 with emissive #ffc98a; doors present.
  - Camera unchanged at `[-20, 15.5, 27] fov 41`; scene bbox identical to the asset it replaces, so nothing in `cameraPath.ts` moves.
  - **0 console errors, 0 warnings.** 71-81 fps against the old asset's 102 on the dev build — the cost is 267 extra nodes, i.e. the masonry itself, and it clears 60 fps with room.
  - The PRODUCTION build's frame rate could not be measured here and is not claimed: both assets read 1.0-1.3 fps on it, identically, and the cause is the harness, not the app. Reducing the viewport 9x (1424x900 -> 464x320) changed nothing, so it is not render cost; and rAF ticks 3 times per 2 s while setTimeout ticks 136 times, a 45x gap. The browser window had become occluded and Chromium throttled animation frames. The GPU is real (ANGLE / AMD Radeon / D3D11), not SwiftShader. Anyone re-running this must check rAF against setTimeout before believing an FPS figure.
  - Visual QA at hero, west 6 m, east 6 m, north 6 m, corner 6 m, entrance and south 12 m, driving the app's own scene and lights with the frameloop frozen. Coursing, joints, reveals, keystones, rusticated base, corner quoins and the full entrance composition all read, and the wall and the blocks read as ONE stone — the world-UV fix holding at runtime.
- Verified: typecheck exit 0; lint exit 0 (warnings only, none in the experience code); 48/48 vitest pass including the 29 in `cameraPath.test.ts` that assert the path against the model's solid volumes.
- NOT verified, and worth saying: the scroll-driven journey. SmoothScroll runs a virtual scroll that does not respond to synthetic `scrollTo` or `wheel` events, so the camera could not be driven along the path under Playwright. The hero beat is the app's own camera and was checked directly; the rest of the path is unchanged code against an identical bounding box.
- Flagged for review: **no** — but two standing notes. (a) `?model=prod` is the rollback and `exterior_mansion.glb` must not be deleted. (b) 9.72 MB against 5.62 MB is honest cost: production predates the five-material stone system and carried 13 textures against 26.

## [2026-08-28 08:15 IST] Runtime colour parity: the asset was never the cause

- Reported symptom: "the live website still visually shows the old colour/material appearance" against the Blender artist's approved renders. Traced before changing anything. The asset is not the cause and nothing in the material pipeline needed fixing.

- **The default route was already serving v5, and that is now proven from the built artefact rather than from source.** `EXTERIOR_MODEL_URL = '/models/exterior_mansion_v5.glb'`; no env var, `next.config`, CDN prefix, rewrite or preload overrides it; the only other mentions of the old filename in `apps/public/src` are two comments. Grepping the production chunks finds all three paths baked in (v5 default, prod rollback, etc1s candidate). From a FRESH browser context on the production build, the only model request is `/models/exterior_mansion_v5.glb`, HTTP 200, and the bytes the server returns hash to `d7a7e945…bc87c3`, identical to the file on disk. `legacyRequested: false` — no stale fallback, no second request.

- **Every layer of the material system survives into the runtime.** Measured live in three.js, not inferred:
  - `baseColorFactor` -> `material.color` = **#e7e3da**, linear (0.7991, 0.7682, 0.7011) — the locked tint.
  - roughness 1, metalness 0, `map.colorSpace` srgb, `normalMap.colorSpace` srgb-linear, 1024px with 11 mip levels.
  - `vertexColors: true`, COLOR_0 present as Float32 itemSize 3, min 0.400 max 1.000 — the AO payload, live on 408 meshes against the old asset's 1.
  - 267 ashlar, 96 rustic, 479 meshes, 179,397 triangles.

- **The texture is not shifted by the pipeline.** The shipped KTX2 was extracted from the GLB, decoded with `ktx extract`, and compared against Blender's `marble026/color.png`: mean sRGB (196.3, 172.3, 137.7) against (195.3, 172.7, 136.7) — **max channel delta 1.05/255, 0.41%**. Effective albedo (tint x texture, linear) is (0.4433, 0.3186, 0.1781) shipped against (0.4382, 0.3203, 0.1751) in Blender.

- **`normalScale` reads [1.2, -1.2] and that is CORRECT, not a defect.** three's GLTFLoader flips the green channel only under `useDerivativeTangents` — a normal map on geometry with no TANGENT attribute, where tangents come from screen-space derivatives. Our primitives ship POSITION/NORMAL/TEXCOORD_0/COLOR_0 and no TANGENT, so the flip fires by design (mrdoob/three.js#11438).

- **THE ACTUAL CAUSE: render transform and lighting rig, both authored, neither an asset property.**
  - three.js renders **ACESFilmic**; Blender's approved renders use **AgX**. Swept live at REV_WEST6 on the identical frame: Linear [87,78,64] L78.8 / Reinhard L75.1 / Cineon L79.2 / **ACES [84,72,55] L73.3** / **AgX [94,84,72] L85.6** / Neutral L59.5. ACES against AgX is +17% luma and a visible saturation shift (R-B of 29 against 22).
  - The site lights a **dusk rig** — key #FFB264 at 2.3 with shadows, counter #7FB4D6 at 0.3, hemisphere #4A6B96/#1A1512 at 0.36, ambient 0.06, `environmentIntensity` 0.45, fog #0A1120 from 26-105 m. The Blender review renders used a neutral sun (1.0, 0.94, 0.86) with a daylight HDRI at exposure -0.9.
  - **The stone pigment is identical.** Sampling mid-tone stone in both, Blender reads hue **34.3 deg**, sat 0.131, light 0.477; the live site reads hue **34.6 deg**, sat 0.240, light 0.300. **Hue delta 0.3 deg.** Same limestone, rendered darker and more saturated by a dusk rig and a contrastier curve.

- **Why it reads as "the old appearance".** Through the dusk rig the two assets differ by only 2-10% in mean frame luma: hero 66.55 -> 71.10 (+6.8%), west6 73.02 -> 74.35, corner6 110.61 -> 117.93, entrance 151.68 -> 154.51, east6 105.61 -> 107.39, north6 130.92 -> 143.95 (+10.0%), south12 125.86 -> 131.53. The assets ARE materially different — the old wall is `color #ffffff` with `vertexColors false`, v5 is `#e7e3da` with AO on 408 meshes — but the lighting compresses that difference at hero distance, where 25 mm of block relief is also about one pixel.

- Legitimate production benchmark, window verified active first (rAF 97/s against setTimeout 52/s): **90.7 fps** mean of 89.0 / 90.3 / 92.7 at 1440x900. JS heap 88.5 MB, DCL 523 ms, GLB fetch 78 ms. The earlier ~1 fps figure was an occluded-window rAF throttle and is void.
- Verified: production build exit 0 (BUILD_ID klPBiKW-VAu5sBcK26dJN); all three assets HTTP 200 with correct byte counts; console 0 errors / 0 warnings.
- Flagged for review: **yes, one art-direction fork.** Moving the renderer from ACESFilmic to AgX would bring the site materially closer to the approved Blender tonality, but it is a SITE-WIDE colour-management change — it desaturates the gold, the typography accent and the whole interior too, and WorldCanvas's exposure notes are written against the ACES curve. Not taken unilaterally.

## [2026-09-01 06:50 IST] Visual parity: the asset was right, the GRADE was the difference — and it is now addressable

- The prior pass closed on "COLOR PARITY: PASS" from a 0.3 degree stone-hue delta. That conclusion was overruled and the overrule was correct: technical asset parity is not visual parity. A 0.3 degree hue match on a sampled pigment says the two renders use the same limestone. It says nothing about whether the two IMAGES look alike, and they did not.

- **The camera question was a real confound and it is settled.** Blender `REV_HERO` sits at loc (-20,-27,15.5), lens 32.1 mm, vfov 41 deg, which converts to three-space (-20, 15.5, 27) fov 41 — EXACTLY the website hero camera. The Blender still I had previously put beside the site was rendered from `P3_CAM` (-26,-30,12) at 45 mm / 29.86 deg, a diagnostic camera created during the geometry sweep. Part of the mismatch the client saw was me comparing two different lenses. Re-rendered from REV_HERO before any conclusion was drawn.

- **Blender's conditions were READ from the scene, not inferred.** BLENDER_EEVEE; view transform AgX, look "AgX - Base Contrast", exposure 0.0 (the -0.9 in an earlier session was mine, not the artist's); world `W_HDRI` on `199_hdrmaps_com_free_2K.exr` at strength 1.0; `SUN_KEY` energy 3.2, colour (1, 0.93, 0.84), rotation (55.1, 0, 63.9) = 34.9 deg elevation.

- **ACES vs AgX was NOT the cause, and I had over-weighted it.** Ladder run live against the Blender ground truth (frame mean, 9 sampled regions, frameloop frozen, readPixels in the same task as the render):

    ```
        A  site as shipped                     63.9
        C  AgX view transform only             69.2   (+8%)
        D  exposure 2.2 only                   95.4   (background still 16.6)
        E  environment/sky only               145.5   (background 180.1)
        F  combined                           148.8
        -- Blender REV_HERO ground truth      101.1
    ```

    Swapping the view transform moves the frame 8%. The ENVIRONMENT moves it 128%. In the tuned grid ACES outscored AgX in every row and the two worst rows were both AgX, so the shipped tone mapping is kept unchanged. The dusk rig — a navy sky, fog from 34 m, a warm backlight and a 0.36 hemisphere — was the whole difference.

- **The brief does not mandate dusk.** masterprompt.md:428 asks for a "premium dusk/evening or editorial daylight grade depending on what works best with the actual asset". Both are in scope, which is why this ships as a fork rather than as a silent replacement.

- **Implemented as an addressable grade, default unchanged.** `?grade=daylight` extends the existing per-set `LOOK` / `useLook` mechanism that already carries `?exposure=&env=&ambient=` — no new architecture. Fitted by minimising RMS against the REV_HERO render across nine regions; best fit RMS 16.9. Sun placed where SUN_KEY actually is (34.9 deg elevation converted Blender (x,y,z) -> three (x,z,-y)); env 0.45->0.7, exposure 1.0->0.75, ambient 0.06->0.2, hemisphere 0.36->0.8, sky #0A1120->#6D7F6A, fog off, cool counter-key off.

- **Scoped by construction, and proven, not argued.** `<ExteriorLighting>` and the graded background only mount for `set === 'exterior'`, and `LOOK` was already per-set. Runtime proof: the interior route `/knowledge/reading-a-layout-plan` rendered with and without `?grade=daylight` is **pixel-identical — max absolute difference 0/255 across the full 1440x900 frame.**

- **The typography cost is real, smaller than I first measured, and it lands on ONE element.** Measured against a backdrop plate (the frame re-rendered with glyphs transparent, so the background is read rather than guessed between letterforms) — an earlier per-row estimate was measuring glyph pixels and produced false failures. Corrected figures, typical/worst backdrop:

    ```
                          DUSK          DAYLIGHT      AA needs
        H1 (66px)      12.87 / 7.26    7.53 / 5.62      3.0
        body (16.8px)   4.29 / 3.89    4.69 / 3.86      4.5
        gold link       5.33 / 4.21    5.35 / 4.81      4.5
        secondary link  3.55 / 3.23    3.72 / 3.37      4.5
        eyebrow         5.49 / 5.47    3.69 / 2.93      4.5
    ```

    A left-column scrim (0.38 at the edge, gone by 52% of frame width, so the building is never dimmed) pays most of it. Daylight ends up BETTER than dusk on body copy, gold and the secondary link, and worse on exactly one: the eyebrow, 5.49 -> 3.69.

- **DUSK IS NOT ACCESSIBLE EITHER, and the first version of my own code comment claimed it was.** On the shipping grade body copy reads 4.29 and the secondary link 3.55, both under AA. That is a pre-existing defect of 0.55/0.70-alpha type over a lit lawn, it is not caused by daylight, and the comment has been corrected to say so. The real fix is opacity on the small type, which is a typography change and outside this pass.

- Verified: typecheck exit 0; lint exit 0 (pre-existing warnings only, none in WorldCanvas); 48/48 vitest including the 29 cameraPath assertions; production build exit 0; 0 console errors and 0 warnings on both grades; default route still serves `exterior_mansion_v5.glb` (9433 KB, 360 ms) with no query parameter. Frame rate, rAF gated against setTimeout first (rAF 87-90/s, not throttled): **dusk 88.4 fps, daylight 89.9 fps** at 1424x900 — daylight is marginally FASTER, having no fog and one fewer contributing light.
- NOT verified: the scroll-driven journey past the hero beat. SmoothScroll's virtual scroll does not respond to synthetic wheel/scrollTo under automation, unchanged from the previous pass. The daylight grade alters no camera, no geometry and no bounding box, so `cameraPath` is untouched.
- Flagged for review: **yes — one art-direction decision, deliberately not taken unilaterally.** Daylight matches the approved Blender render and reveals the masonry, the slate roof and the site that dusk hides; dusk is what every lighting comment in WorldCanvas is written against and what the scrims were tuned for. The default remains dusk. Flipping it is one line in `GRADE_LOOK` plus making `'daylight'` the `useState` seed.

## [2026-09-01 07:50 IST] Runtime visual-polish pass: terrace, sky, windows

- Three residual gaps were named in the previous entry and left as follow-ups. This pass closes all three at RUNTIME. No Blender edit, no re-export, no GLB, no masonry, no UV, no AO, no exporter change. The v5 asset is untouched.

- **Measured the same way as the parity pass**: raw canvas with every DOM layer hidden, against the approved REV_HERO render, because the reference carries no typography or scrim and comparing the composited page would measure the UI instead of the grade.

    ```
                      Blender    before     after
        facade          115.4     123.9     121.6   (+6.2)
        roof slate      111.9     113.6     113.2   (+1.3)
        terrace         134.6     165.6     139.6   (+5.0)
        lawn             99.3     100.1     100.0   (+0.7)
        fountain         63.8      56.6      54.8   (-9.0)
        background       53.9      99.1      62.4   (+8.5)
        spire            95.1     103.7     103.4   (+8.3)
        ---------------------------------------------------
        RMS luma error             21.4       6.4
    ```

- **1. TERRACE — 165.6 -> 139.6, and the facade did not move.** `PAVING_TINT` becomes per-grade: dusk keeps 0.48, daylight takes 0.32. The 0.48 was argued from a dusk sky — a horizontal plate sees a near-black sky while a wall sees the key — and that argument survives into daylight but the number does not, because the sky is now a light source the plate sees more of. The facade is a different material after the stone split, so darkening the paving cannot touch it: measured 123.9 -> 121.6, i.e. it did not move. Swept, not derived.

- **2. BACKGROUND — the flat colour was structurally incapable of being right.** Sampling the reference down its left margin shows the background is not sky: a 3/4 bird's-eye camera puts the horizon high and fills the top of frame with distant LAND — dark treeline L 44..48 to y=50, breaking into sunlit field L 112..117 by y=100. A single colour is necessarily too bright against the treeline and too dark against the field, and `#6D7F6A` was +45 and -16 respectively. Replaced with a 2x512 vertical ramp built on a canvas at runtime (no asset, no fetch, nothing for the CSP to refuse) assigned to `scene.background`, which three renders as a fullscreen quad for any non-env texture. Profile error after three iterations: -4.8, +0.1, +8.5, +1.6, -0.8, -0.6, +1.4, +3.7 across y 0..175. **Background only** — `scene.environment` is still the generated cube map, so this changes what is behind the building and lights nothing.
  - Stops are pre-compensated for ACES at exposure 0.75 and read brighter in source than the targets they hit. That is why they look wrong if read as colours rather than as pre-tone-map values.
  - A measurement error of mine is recorded here because it nearly sent the tuning backwards: the `bg_upper` sample region was placed when the background was FLAT, at a y-band that did not match the reference's. Once the background was graduated the region compared our open field against Blender's treeline and reported the better ramp as worse (RMS 11.6 -> 20.6). The region was corrected to the reference's own band and the ranking inverted to what the vertical profile had said all along.

- **3. WINDOWS — the largest remaining architectural mismatch, and it was two systems, not one.** The reference shows dark recessed glazing; ours glowed. Both contributors are now grade-gated:
  - `EMISSIVE` splits per grade. Daylight sets `MAT_Window_Interior` and `MAT_Wood_Dark` to 0. Every argument for the dusk values is an argument about darkness — the bloom threshold, the dark end of the orbit, a facade that would read as abandoned — and none of them apply at midday. `MAT_Gold` keeps a token 0.08: not for glow, since at metalness 1 against a 0.7 environment it has plenty to reflect, but because the finials are 40mm details at hero distance and zero loses the roofline.
  - The interior-spill rig in WorldCanvas is extinguished for daylight: both flanking rect-area lights and both point lights to 0, the entry bay held at 0.25 in a neutral `#FFE2C4` because the portico is the one place the key genuinely cannot reach under its own overhang.
  - The `__graded` guard became `__gradedFor: Grade`. `useGLTF` caches the parse and `scene.clone(true)` SHARES materials with it, so a sticky boolean would have pinned whichever grade mounted first.

- Verified on a fresh build (BUILD_ID R6wAgfc-CA5uipbcnF95M) and a fresh server and browser context: typecheck 0; lint 0 (pre-existing warnings only, none in the experience code); 48/48 vitest; production build 0; **0 console errors, 0 warnings** across default, `?grade=dusk` and the interior route.
- **Regression proofs.** DUSK rollback, canvas only, after vs before this pass: mean **0.043/255**, and the 0.199% of pixels that differ are spread over 84 of 160 grid cells with no cell holding more than 2.9% — scattered animation noise from the 2,400-particle motes field, not a cluster, so nothing structural moved. INTERIOR before vs after: mean **0.030/255, max 2/255, zero pixels over 8.**
- **Frame rate, and an honest caveat about the absolute number.** On this build, same conditions, back to back: **daylight 67.7 fps, dusk 65.1** — daylight remains the faster of the two, as it was before this pass (90.4 / 88.7). The absolute figures are ~25% below the earlier session on the same hardware; the rAF gate tracks the render rate exactly in both, and the change removes four lights rather than adding any, so the drop is accumulated machine load across a long session and NOT attributable to this work. Do not compare absolute FPS across sessions; compare the two grades within one.
- Flagged for review: the fountain sits 9 luma under the reference because the warm uplight that used to carry it is off. Restoring a warm light on a fountain at midday would be chasing a number at the expense of the image, so it is left. The remaining +6..8 on facade and spire are small and were not pursued for the same reason.

## [2026-09-02 19:31 IST] Phase 2.5B closed: the roof's lost base-colour multipliers, and a measurement basis that was wrong before it
- Found, and the order matters: **the comparison basis was invalid, so the defect it reported was not the defect that exists.** The Blender reference camera reproduced this page's `frameOffset` by crossing the forward vector with Blender's Z-up. three.js crosses with its own Y-up, and converting three (x,y,z) -> blender (x,-z,y) turns three's `right` into `(-fz, +fx, 0)` while the Z-up cross gives `(-fz, -fx, 0)` — **mirrored**, so the aim was pushed the wrong way and the building sat ~50px off. An edge-correlation alignment had returned dx=0/dy=0 and was believed; it was dominated by the hills and hedges, which are far away and therefore barely moved. Restricting the correlation to the building window exposes it.
- Why it matters: every roof number reported before this was measured between two differently-framed images through hand-placed boxes, one of which straddled the roof/cornice boundary and sampled cream stone. That is where "the roof is the second-brightest major surface" and "1.86x" came from. **Both were artefacts.** On a valid basis the roof is 5th of 11 and the ratio is 1.47.
- Fix applied, measurement side: camera reconstruction moved into three-space and converted afterwards (`right = cross(fwd, (0,1,0))`), and regions are no longer boxes at all — a **material-index pass** renders every material as a flat emission encoding its index in red, box filter width 0.01, Raw view transform, 1 sample, so the PNG's red channel is the material index exactly. Both images are sampled through that mask, eroded 3px to drop antialiased edges.
- Fix applied, defect side: `tools/gltf/bake_lost_basecolor.py`, and the candidate `exterior_mansion_v6_p25b.glb` (registered as `?model=p25b`, **not** promoted). Blender's slate base colour is `texture x (0.82, 0.86, 0.94) x (0.5 + 0.5 x AO)`, written as two legacy `ShaderNodeMixRGB` nodes. The exporter reads a base-colour chain only through the newer `ShaderNodeMix`, so it walked past both and wrote `baseColorFactor [1,1,1,1]` — 1.31x too bright in linear light. `fix_stone_material_export.py` had already converted the five `MAT_Stone_*` materials for this exact reason; the roof was never in its hardcoded list. Both multipliers are baked into a derived texture in linear light. `f=0.5` is half-strength AO and is baked as such.
- Verified:
  1. **Alignment, three independent ways.** Building-window NCC peaks at **dx=0, dy=0**; spire tip REF y=174 vs runtime y=173; mansion left edge 606 vs 606.
  2. **The camera was settled, not caught mid-lerp.** Two captures 6s apart differ by mean **0.046/255**, and the 0.13% of pixels that move are all below y=328 — the animated water. rAF was confirmed firing first; the in-app browser pane throttles it to zero when hidden and cannot produce a settled capture at all.
  3. **The arithmetic closes on a control, which is what makes it a diagnosis.** `MAT_Roof` carries the same slate texture with **no** multipliers in its graph, so its export was already faithful. At the lit end (p90, in linear) it matches Blender **1.018x** — so the lighting agrees there and any excess is albedo. `MAT_Roof_Slate` at the same end is **1.252x**, against 1.167 predicted from the tint alone (where AO -> 1) and 1.307 from tint x mean AO. It sits between them, where it must.
  4. **The encode is like-for-like, not a second opinion.** Re-encoding the ORIGINAL texture through the same LANCZOS-1024 resize and `ktx create --format R8G8B8_SRGB --encode basis-lz --qlevel 255` reproduces the bytes v5 already ships — identical length and sha256. The script asserts this and refuses to run if it ever stops being true.
  5. **The candidate is a one-texture edit, proven structurally.** 479 nodes, 381 meshes, 1785 accessors and the first **9,863,656 bytes** of the buffer are byte-identical to v5; `MAT_Roof_Slate` is the only material that differs. Running the committed tool on v5 reproduces the shipped candidate byte for byte (sha256 `643f0690…e914d3`).
  6. **Runtime A/B, daylight HERO.** Roof **74.5 -> 70.2** luma against Blender's 50.8; 95.9% of all changed pixels are inside the roof mask and 96.2% within a 3px collar of it, the remainder being the water band already shown to move between two frames of the same model. **Dusk HERO:** roof 104.1 -> 100.5, and `MAT_Stone_Wall`, `MAT_Stone_Trim`, `MAT_Stone_Paving`, `MAT_Ground` and `MAT_Roof` all move by **±0.01 or less**.
  7. typecheck 0; 48/48 vitest; lint 0 (pre-existing warnings only — the `ExteriorModel` one is a `useEffect` dep at line 619, untouched); production build 0; 0 console errors and 0 warnings. v5 still hashes `d7a7e945…bc87c3`, p34 is untouched, `EXTERIOR_MODEL_URL` still points at v5, and the locked source blend still has mtime 2026-08-27 22:43:35.
- **Two things found and deliberately NOT fixed**, both with numbers rather than opinions:
  1. **`MAT_Wood_Dark`'s lost multiplier points the other way.** `MIX` at Fac 0.68 against a constant (0.255, 0.165, 0.1) makes Blender's albedo **3.30x brighter** than the shipped texture — while the runtime already renders those doors **3.2x brighter** than Blender (41.0 vs 12.9). The two errors are in opposition. Baking it would take the doors to **76.5** and triple the error. The dominant term is missing contact occlusion on a deeply recessed door, not albedo. This is why the mandate's own instruction not to fix a mismatch before identifying its cause earns its place: the named fix was the wrong fix for that surface.
  2. **`MAT_Roof_Slate` loses its roughness remap too, and that is the rest of the gap.** Blender feeds the roughness map through a Map Range to **0.42..0.82** (LINEAR interpolation, so the Steps=4 field is inert); the exporter cannot express Map Range either, so it emitted the raw map with the default factor 1.0. **85.1% of the roof renders glossier than authored**, floor 0.118 against 0.467, mean 0.569 against 0.647. Measured on the shipped file, only **47.9%** of the roof's radiance is modulated by base colour — the other 52.1% is specular and IBL, which is why an albedo-only correction moves 74.5 to 70.2 and not to the 65.2 a pure multiply would predict. Fixing it is the same technique in a different slot (bake the remap into a derived metallicRoughness map) and is a separate authorisation.
- Flagged for review: **yes.** (a) The candidate is registered, not promoted — `EXTERIOR_MODEL_URL` still resolves v5 and the swap is a one-line change. (b) The roof closes 18% of its gap (+23.7 -> +19.4) and moves from 5th to 6th of 11 in the luminance hierarchy where Blender places it 7th; the remaining gap is the roughness export loss above plus the runtime's lack of ray-traced occlusion, which shows up on **every** material (`MAT_Stone_Trim`, with no export loss at all, still runs p10 28 -> 54) and is not something a base-colour edit can reach. (c) The blend and the GLB now diverge: the node graph still carries the legacy MixRGB chain, so a future re-export from source would lose this again unless it runs through the new tool. That is why the recipe is a committed script with a self-check rather than a one-off.

## [2026-09-03 13:55 IST] P2.5B.2: the roof's lost roughness remap - and two measurement instruments that were both wrong
- Found: `MAT_Roof_Slate` feeds its roughness map through a `Map Range` node, 0..1 -> **0.42..0.82**, LINEAR (so the `Steps=4` field is inert) and clamped. Map Range is no more exportable than the legacy MixRGB nodes P2.5B dealt with, so v5 shipped the raw map with the default factor: **85.2% of the roof renders glossier than authored**, floor 0.188 against 0.489, and a ceiling of 1.000 against an authored hard limit of 0.82.
- Why a `roughnessFactor` is NOT the fix, which was the obvious wrong move: glTF roughness is `factor x texture.G`, a pure MULTIPLY; Blender's remap is AFFINE. No factor can produce the 0.42 floor. The offset has to be baked into the texture, which leaves only the question of where the CEILING lives - and that is settled by measurement, not preference. Round-tripped through the real encoder and compared per texel against the float ideal:

  | representation | mean abs err | rms | max | resulting range |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | v5 as shipped | 0.11218 | 0.12363 | 0.309 | 0.188 .. **1.000** |
  | factor 1.00, whole span in G | 0.00869 | 0.01274 | 0.135 | 0.498 .. **0.847** |
  | **factor 0.82, G normalised** | **0.00815** | **0.01231** | **0.131** | 0.486 .. **0.820** |

  Factor 1.0 lets ETC1S noise reach **0.847, above a ceiling Map Range's clamp makes hard**; putting 0.82 in the factor caps it by construction and keeps 104 levels of 8-bit instead of 85. The remap is applied AFTER the pipeline resize: it is affine and LANCZOS is linear with unit-sum weights so the orders agree in float, but resizing first carries the full range through the filter and only then compresses it.
- Fix applied: `tools/gltf/bake_lost_material_maps.py` (renamed from `bake_lost_basecolor.py`, now covering both slots) and the candidate `exterior_mansion_v6_p25b2.glb`, registered as `?model=p25b2` and **not promoted**. Both controls hold: re-encoding the ORIGINAL base colour AND the ORIGINAL packed metallicRoughness through the same resize and `ktx create` reproduces the bytes v5 ships, sha256 and all. The packing was read out of the shipped file rather than assumed - decoding image 22 gives R=255, **G=roughness (mean 0.5684, matching the source's 0.569)**, B=255. The script asserts both controls and a VERIFY pass, and refuses to write if any drifts.
- **Two instruments were wrong before the numbers meant anything.** Both are worth recording because both produced confident, plausible, false readings.
  1. **The Blender material mask is not the runtime's material footprint.** An unlit-magenta probe of `MAT_Roof_Slate` shows the runtime draws it on 42,762 px where Blender's mask claims 51,352 - **16.9% of the mask is not that material in the runtime at all**, and those pixels sit at mean luma 104.6 against the true roof's 61.0. Every roof figure sampled through the Blender mask alone was contaminated by them, which is why p90/p95/p99 came out *identical to 0.1 luma across three different models*: those pixels could not respond to a change in a material they do not have. On the intersection basis the roof reads 44.0 in Blender, not 50.8.
  2. **WEST and NW were never on their beats.** `WorldCanvas` eases the curve parameter as `curveT(SWING(s))` but reads fov, roll and frameOffset at **raw `s`** (lines 342 vs 345/394). Two consequences: scrolling to a beat's `at` does NOT stand on that beat - WEST needs legProgress **0.408591** and NW **0.532700**, since `SWING(s)=0.2s+0.8*power2.inOut(s)` and GSAP's power2 is CUBIC, not quadratic - and even there the lens is wrong, because at those scrolls `lensAt` returns fov 54.4487/offset 7.5019 for WEST (the beat says 56/8.2) and 52.6757/6.7041 for NW (52/6.4). **A beat's position and its own lens are never on screen together except at s=0 and s=1.** The references were re-rendered at the lens the runtime actually uses.
- Verified, all beats at 1424x900 against Cycles references framed by reproducing this page's own camera maths:
  - **HERO daylight**, on the runtime-verified footprint (Blender mask AND probe, 35,061 px): Blender 44.0; v5 **69.4** (+25.4, MAE 26.7); p25b 64.3 (+20.4, MAE 21.7); p25b2 **61.9** (+18.0, MAE **19.5**, RMS 24.4). p90 83 -> 77 -> **74** against Blender's 68 - the bright end responds once the contaminated pixels are out, which is exactly where a roughness change should act.

The fix is `Recalculate Outside` on `mansion_roof` in the source; it is geometry authoring, not material authoring, and is out of scope here. Setting `doubleSided:true` would hide it but would contradict the authored flag and paper over the real bug, so it was not done.

Of that, the misoriented-normal hole is the single largest remaining term; what is left after it is the runtime's lack of ray-traced occlusion, which hits every material (`MAT_Stone_Trim`, with no export loss at all, still runs p10 28 -> 54) and is renderer-inherent. (c) The camera finding in instrument #2 is a real inconsistency in `WorldCanvas` - the vantage `curveT`'s own docstring promises is never the one on screen - but the camera is locked, so it is recorded and not touched.

## [2026-09-03 14:35 IST] Phase 2.5B closed: the roof's third defect was the mesh, and the first two measurements were taken through it
- Found: after the base-colour and roughness fixes the roof still read +17.4, and the top decile of it would not move for either fix - identical to 0.1 luma across v5, p25b and p25b2. A lit surface cannot ignore both its albedo and its roughness. It was not ignoring them: **16.9% of the pixels being measured were not that material.** An unlit-magenta probe of MAT_Roof_Slate showed the runtime drawing it over 42,762 px of the 51,352 px Blender assigns it at HERO, and a full 15-material probe identified 96.6% of the 8,684 px difference as MAT_Stone_Wall. The viewer was seeing THROUGH the roof to the wall behind it.
- Why it matters twice over: it is a visible hole in the hero shot, and every roof number in the two entries above was averaged over a region that was 17% cream stone. The corrected figures are in the table below.
- Cause, established rather than assumed. Not z-fighting: ray-casting those pixels puts the slate in front by a **median 4.83 m**, with only 0.4% of samples having a second surface within a millimetre. 246 of 250 rays hit `mansion_roof` on a **BACK-facing** polygon - which for a first intersection from outside a closed surface is impossible unless the winding is wrong. Recalculate-outside flips **936 of its 1873 faces** and the mesh carries 356 non-manifold edges. Cycles shades whichever side a ray lands on, so the Blender reference never showed it. `MAT_Roof_Slate.use_backface_culling` is True, so the exporter wrote `doubleSided: false` **correctly** - three.js was right to cull, and the export was faithful. The defect is in the mesh.
- Fix applied: `normals_make_consistent(inside=False)` on `mansion_roof` alone, in a working copy saved as `mansion_exterior_P25B2-NORMALS.blend`. The locked source is never opened for writing. The script asserts every other mesh's polygon normals are bit-identical afterwards and that vertex positions are untouched - this is an orientation fix, not a remodel.
- **NOT fixed by setting `doubleSided: true`**, which was the tempting one-line answer. That hides the symptom and leaves the tangent basis the normal map is sampled in still mirrored across half the roof. The proof that this matters: correcting the winding is **not a no-op in Blender**. The reference roof moves 50.80 -> 49.21 because tangent space follows winding, and every other material moves by 0.000.
- Delivered as `exterior_mansion_v6_p25b3.glb` (`?model=p25b3`), **not promoted**. Transplanted, not re-exported: the shipped file is Draco-compressed and gltf-transform warns a decode/re-encode round trip is lossy, so a full re-export would perturb all 381 meshes to fix one. `tools/gltf/transplant_mesh.py` writes the corrected primitive uncompressed and drops Draco for that primitive only - **380 of 381 primitives keep their original payload byte for byte**, and the first 10,291,240 bytes of the buffer are identical.
- Verified:
  1. **The hole is closed, measured the same way it was found.** Runtime MAT_Roof_Slate footprint at HERO: **42,762 px -> 51,315 px** against a 51,352 px Blender mask. Missing 16.9% -> **0.3%**, and the 132 px remaining are antialiased silhouette.
  2. **Scene-wide back-face sweep**, 80,100 rays on a 4px grid: back-facing first hits **1,286 -> 697**, and `mansion_roof`'s contribution **590 -> 0**.
  3. **The export path was proven before it was trusted.** A fresh export of the UNFIXED mesh reproduces the shipped one: 6,258 verts and 3,746 tris exactly, and every shipped vertex lies within **0.782 mm** of a control vertex - the Draco quantisation step over a 15.8 m bbox. Bounding boxes agree to 0.000000 m.
  4. **HERO daylight, MAT_Roof_Slate, each candidate against the Blender render of the source it was built from:**

     |---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | v5 shipped | 50.8 | 74.5 | +23.7 | 26.1 | 31.0 |
     | p25b +basecolour | 50.8 | 70.2 | +19.4 | 21.9 | 27.7 |
     | p25b2 +roughness | 50.8 | 68.2 | +17.4 | 20.1 | 26.4 |
     | **p25b3 +winding** | **49.2** | **65.7** | **+16.5** | **16.7** | **21.9** |

     Roof MAE **-36%**, RMS **-29%**. Whole-frame MAE 18.40 -> 18.03.

- **First-principles reassessment.** Every material's Base Color and Roughness chain was walked and classified against what the exporter can carry. The five stone materials multiply by a Color Attribute, which IS exportable as `COLOR_0` - and all 314 of their primitives carry it, so that path is faithful. `MAT_Stone_Wall` (+43.0), `MAT_Stone_Steps` (+40.0), `MAT_Stone_Paving` (-25.6) and `MAT_Gold` (+54.3) are therefore **correctly exported**, and their residual is the runtime's lighting model: no path-traced GI, and env 0.25 + hemi 0.18 in place of a full HDRI, which redistributes energy from skylit horizontals (paving darker) onto sun-facing verticals (walls brighter). - Found (STEP 1 inventory, `docs/PHASE4_MATERIAL_AUDIT.md`): `MAT_Stone_Wall` - 29.7% of the building at HERO - sampled `marble026/color|roughness|normalgl`, not the coursed limestone `make_stone_materials.py` authored for it (on disk, bound to nothing). Owner decisions taken: rebuild the wall on an authored limestone; fold the paving grade into materials (P4B).
- Why a SURFACE and not the coursed tile: each of the 267 ashlar blocks is one physical stone (0.855 x 0.335 m, 0.36 m course pitch) with world UVs. A coursed texture prints sub-courses across single stones and its 405 mm pitch beats against the 360 mm geometry. So `tools/gltf/make_limestone_wall.py` authors bed mottle (0.4-1.2 m), faint bedding (80-110 mm, colour only), sparse pores, roughness 0.55-0.81, no joints - and the MESO layer rides **COLOR_0**: `tools/blender/blocktone_stoneao.py` multiplies each block's StoneAO by a seeded per-object tone (0.90-1.08, darker blocks 1.5% warmer). Zero texture cost, class A export. The shipped ashlar COLOR_0 was 1.0 flat (the AO lives on `mansion_walls`), so the donor loses nothing.
- Three iterations, each measured before the next:
  1. **v1** targeted real limestone albedo (0.54, 0.51, 0.44, R/B 1.23). Under this scene's locked cool environment it rendered **blue-grey in Blender (R/B 0.95)** and **+67 over reference in the runtime**: the marble's heavy warmth had been compensating for the sky, and its low albedo (0.34 luma) for the runtime's direct key.   2. **v2** warmed to effective R/B 2.0 and shipped with `uv_scale` written as `KHR_texture_transform`. The runtime showed a dense horizontal stipple across every block that Cycles never showed: the bedding ripple and pore relief in the NORMAL map under a hard grazing key. **v3** moved bedding to colour only, halved pore depth, normal scale 1.2 -> 0.60. Stipple gone.
  3. **v4** albedo x0.93 to land the runtime back at the marble baseline level.
- Diagnosed, not assumed: Blender's wall dropped 71.6 -> 64.2 across the change. Isolation renders show **roughness accounts for -1.7** and the **marble normal map alone for +10.1** (limestone base/rough with the old normal at 1.2 renders 76.4) - a strong high-frequency normal on a grazing-lit face lifts Cycles' mean. - Verified (HERO daylight, mask basis): wall runtime **114.7 -> 117.3** (+2.6), chroma **R/B 1.55 -> 1.40**, per-block variation present (sd 40.4 -> 42.3); every other material within **+/-0.1** except glass +0.76 (it reflects the wall). 93.1% of changed pixels inside the wall mask. ## [2026-09-04 10:15 IST] Phase 5 / P5A: the ground was a sampling-rate defect, not a look — plus a locked P4A source that had silently drifted

- **The defect is arithmetic, and it was 25–47% of every frame.** The shipped ground is one 1024 map clamped across 240 m — **0.2344 m per texel** — while the runtime probe ray-cast the real cameras at **0.0197 m per PIXEL** at HERO and 0.0153 at NW. The map is MAGNIFIED 12–15× across the nearest half of every exterior frame: below mip 0 there is nothing to sample, so the GPU interpolates between texels carrying no information. It also had no normal map and no AO. Re-baking a prettier 240 m map cannot fix that — the frequency is not there.
- **Fix, in three layers.** MICRO: tiling turf and gravel at **6.0 m** (`tools/gltf/make_ground_p5a.py`) = 0.00586 m/texel, a 3.4× *minification* at the near HERO ground (mip ~1.75) instead of a 12× magnification, and still correctly sampled at the horizon. Both sets are normalised so their linear mean equals the authored target, which makes the mip tail — the far field — the intended colour by construction. MESO: **COLOR_0** on the ground's own 2.5 m vertex grid (damp hollows off the real height field, a wear halo against every hardscape footprint, a broad falloff, a 20–60 m mottle that breaks the 6 m tile). MACRO: **`drive_forecourt`**, 1,960 triangles — a gravel annulus (r 3.9–10.6) around the fountain with its inner edge tucked *under* the apron so there is no cut to stair-step, plus an approach band between the hedge ends.
- **The albedo was SOLVED, not chosen, and the first attempt was wrong in a measurable way.** v1 authored (0.086, 0.132, 0.043) and rendered the lawn at **L 81.3 against the mansion's 100.4 — ratio 0.81**, where the shipped ground sat at 0.70. A surface holding 43.7% of the frame at 81% of the hero's value is the mansion losing dominance. A two-point fit of the runtime's own response over the two real renders gives `rendered = a·source + c` with **a = (0.431, 0.465, 0.491), c = (0.0341, 0.0304, 0.0195)** — roughly half the albedo survives on top of a large additive fill, and that additive `c` is exactly why a textbook grass green renders as neutral olive. Solving it for L 72.5 gave (0.044, 0.0912, 0.0259).
- **Verified (HERO daylight, emissive-id mask basis, pose error 0.000 m):** lawn **L 70.56 → 70.51** (unchanged), **R/G 0.929 → 0.828** (olive → green), **sd 14.36 → 15.75**. Drive new at L 94.93 / sd 33.64, against the terrace paving's 116.06 — gravel below dressed limestone, as it should be. **Every other class moved by ≤ 0.02**: hedge 52.28→52.29, fountain 70.67→70.68, terrace 116.06→116.06, masonry 132.89→132.91, mansion 100.38→100.38, roof 76.34→76.32, steps 110.18→110.17, cypress 44.20→44.20. WEST lawn 64.09→65.29 (R/G 0.881→0.810); NW 65.33→66.15 (0.877→0.813); dusk 59.85→64.01 (+4.16, reported). Coverage: terrain 47.07→43.73 with drive 3.29 — the drive occupies exactly the lawn it replaced and nothing else moved.
- **Cost:** 949 draw calls (+2) / 358,482 submitted triangles (+3,920) at HERO — the drive is drawn twice because `MAT_Water`'s transmission pass re-renders the opaque scene, **not** because of the shadow map: `drive_forecourt` is verified `castShadow:false` in the live scene. 181,357 unique triangles (+1,960), 16 runtime materials (+1 net: Lawn and Gravel in, Ground out), GPU texture residency 49.32 → 54.65 MB, GLB 15.38 MB unpruned / **14.28 MB pruned** against p4e's 11.04.
- **Two tooling defects found and fixed on the way.**
  1. **A locked P4A source had silently drifted, and `git status` could not say so.** P5A imported `make_limestone_wall.py` for four noise helpers; that module executes at import and rewrote the wall set — with its DEFAULT mean, which was still v2's (0.588, 0.469, 0.335) because v3 and v4 were produced by passing `--mean` on the command line. `assets/` is gitignored, so nothing would have flagged it. Caught by a new tool, `tools/gltf/verify_source_against_glb.py`, which re-encodes a source through the pipeline and compares it to the KTX2 in the shipped GLB. Restoring `--mean 0.547 0.436 0.312` reproduces img8 of p4e byte for byte (33,567 B, sha256 `44e9f633e6ae45c4…`) — which is also what **proves** the value. That is now the file's default, and all **seven** Phase 4 stone sources are audited MATCH.
  2. **Blender 5.2 exported the ground's single colour attribute twice** (COLOR_0 as UNSIGNED_BYTE, COLOR_1 as UNSIGNED_SHORT) against 312 primitives elsewhere in the same file carrying one. Cause: `MAT_Lawn` had no Color Attribute node, so Blender fell back to writing every layer — and, worse, Blender was therefore rendering the lawn *without* the meso tone the runtime multiplies in. Adding the `ShaderNodeMix` (RGBA, MULTIPLY, Factor 1) COLOR_0 multiply fixed both.
- **New infrastructure:** `tools/gltf/graft_draco_nodes.py` — adds *and* replaces Draco nodes with their materials, textures and images, matching materials by name so a graft cannot fork `MAT_Stone_Trim`, and verifying that every pre-existing bufferView and material comes out byte-identical. `transplant_draco_nodes.py` could only replace, and only with an unchanged attribute set.
- Flagged for review: **yes** — P5A makes the ground/backdrop seam MORE visible, because the lawn now differs from the photographic plate in both hue and structure where it used to differ only in value. That is P5B's subject and is measured, not hidden: the plane's far edge sits at rows 359–431 of 900 at WEST with the daylight fog reaching only 54% at that distance.

## [2026-09-04 11:40 IST] Phase 5 / P5B: the ground edge moves instead of the fog — and the fog sweep says the locked value is already right

- **The defect and its cause, separated.** Rendering the ground alone in white put its far edge at rows 359–431 of 900 at WEST and 366–433 at NW — a hard line across mid-frame against a photographic backdrop. Daylight fog is a static `Fog(#5E6147, 60, 220)`; the plane is ±120 m, so its far edge sits ~146 m from the WEST camera where that band has reached only **(146−60)/(220−60) = 54%**. The edge was half-dissolved when it stopped. Dusk's `Fog(26, 105)` buries the same edge, which is why dusk composites and daylight does not.
- **Fix: move the edge, not the fog.** `ground_plane`'s own boundary loop extruded outward in four rings to a **260 m circular rim** — same object, same material, same UV convention, so no seam to hide and **no new draw call**. 9,409 → 15,455 vertices, 18,432 → 21,504 triangles.
- **The radius and the shape are derived, and the first attempt was wrong in a way the script's own assertion caught.** A ±320 m SQUARE rim clears the fog at its nearest point (266 m) but throws its corners to **486 m — past the 400 m far plane**, where the clip would have drawn a fresh hard edge. A square cannot satisfy both bounds, its corners being √2 further out than its sides. A circular rim's distance varies only by the camera's own offset from the origin, so **r = 260** clears both at all three cameras: nearest **226.4 / 233.9 / 235.8 m** against a fog completing at 220, farthest **293.6 / 286.1 / 284.2 m** against a 400 m far plane. Asserted per camera over 720 directions.
- **The right acceptance measurement is the STEP, not the edge's position.** Ground at infinity projects to the horizon line, so ground at 146 m and at 260 m both land within a few pixels of it — extending the plane moved the WEST silhouette's top row from 359 to **357**. Two pixels. Judged on position P5B would read as a failure. `tools/capture/phase5_seam.mjs` instead walks each column of the ground's own silhouette and measures the luminance and hue step between a band inside the edge and a band outside it. **Its first version was contaminated** and the numbers said so: the five worst WEST columns were 1116–1172, which is the mansion standing above the far ground — so the probe now renders a second all-geometry mask and rejects any column whose outside band is not true backdrop (137 of 356 rejected at WEST).
- **Measured, contamination removed:** mean step **HERO 18.59 → 16.02 (−13.8%)**, **WEST 26.47 → 22.84 (−13.7%)**, **NW 20.08 → 19.18 (−4.5%)**; WEST hue step **0.0972 → 0.0526 (−46%)**. Real, and partial.
- **The residual has a different cause, and the stop-condition sweep says DO NOT change the locked fog.** At WEST the fully-fogged ground now reads L 81.6 against a backdrop of L 60.1 — the edge is no longer a gradient ending early, it is the fog COLOUR against a darker plate. `tools/capture/phase5_fogsweep.mjs` swept five haze values on the live scene and restored the original (verified `#5e6147, 60, 220` afterwards). The backdrop's own horizon luminance is **HERO 74.7, WEST 60.1, NW 69.3** — it varies by 15 luma with azimuth because it is a photograph. So the sweep trades cameras against each other: `#3E4238` takes WEST from 22.67 to **14.57** and NW to 16.08, but takes HERO from 16.02 to **21.16**, monotonically worse. There is no single fog colour that improves all three, and the shipped value is already the optimum for the frame the site opens on. **Left unchanged, on evidence.**
- Flagged for review: **yes** — the residual seam is inherent to compositing a single-colour fog against a photographic backdrop whose horizon brightness varies with azimuth. Closing it properly needs per-direction haze (fog colour sampled from the background), which is a shader change to a locked system and outside Phase 5's remit. Reported, not hidden.
- Cost: +3,072 triangles, **+0 draw calls, +0 materials, +0 textures**. GLB 14.35 MB pruned (p5a 14.28).

Both move to 1.5 m tiling sets (`tools/gltf/make_foliage_p5.py`) with box-projected world-scale UVs, and the factors drop to (1,1,1).

Leaf detail would put all the energy below every camera's Nyquist limit and buy sparkle. The sets carry 150–400 mm clump structure, the shear plane a clipped hedge shows on its cut faces, and — for the cypress — a noise lattice stretched **3:1 along v** because a cypress's sprays run up the tree.

At NW where the cypress is largest (4.08%): sd **10.94 → 14.95, +37%**. At dusk: cypress sd **9.14 → 12.16, +33%**. **Every unrelated class moved ≤ 0.04** at every camera — terrain +0.02, terrace +0.02, mansion +0.02, masonry +0.01, roof 0.00, steps +0.03, fountain +0.01, drive +0.04. Luminance essentially unchanged, surface where there was none.
- **Deliberately NOT changed, with the reason measured.** The hedge geometry: a clipped hedge's batter is ~40 mm over a 1.0 m height, which is **four pixels**, and buying it would cost a bounds waiver and a re-ship for detail no camera resolves. The open FRONT of the boundary between the two hedge runs' ends: that gap is the arrival, and P5A's forecourt runs through it.
